Other components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_{ij}^{l*}$$\end{document} are obtained via the symmetry relations from Eqs. 44 to 46, with all other values being zero.

The general formulation of the linear stiffness matrix for the (DPM) can be written as:47\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ K \right] = \frac{{{{(N + 1)}^3}{E_m}{I_0}}}{{{L^3}}}{\hspace{1.0pt}} \left[ {\begin{array}{*{20}{c}} {k_{11}^{l*}}& {k_{12}^{l*}}& {k_{13}^*}& 0& 0& \ldots & .& \ldots & 0& 0\\ {k_{21}^{l*}}& {k_{22}^{l*}}& {k_{23}^{l*}}& \ddots & 0& \ddots & .& .& .& .\\ {k_{31}^{l*}}& {k_{32}^{l*}}& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& 0& .& \vdots & \vdots \\ 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& 0& \vdots & \vdots \\ 0& 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& \vdots \\ .& .& 0& .& .& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& 0\\ \vdots & .& 0& \ddots & .& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0\\ .& \vdots & .& \ddots & 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & {k_{N - 2N}^{l*}}\\ .& .& .& .& .& 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & {k_{N - 1N}^{l*}}\\ 0& 0& \ldots & .& .& 0& 0& {k_{NN-2}^{l*}} & {k_{NN - 1}^{l*}}& {k_{NN}^{l*}} \end{array}} \right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Consequently:48\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [K] = \frac{{E_m I_0 (N + 1)^3}}{{L^3}} [K^*] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[K^*]$$\end{document} indicates the dimensionless stiffness matrix pertaining to the discrete system.

The stiffness tensor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k^f_{ij}$$\end{document}

The stiffness tensor for the elastic foundation is defined as48:49\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k_{ij}^f =l k_i^{sf} \delta _{ij} = \frac{k_i^{sf*} E_m I_0}{(N + 1) L^3} \delta _{ij} \quad \text {for} \quad i,j = 1, \ldots , N \end{aligned}$$\end{document}With \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_r^{sf*}$$\end{document} taking the values \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (1 - \alpha x_r^*)^2$$\end{document} for (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=1$$\end{document}) or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta (1 + \sin (\alpha x_r^*))$$\end{document} for ( \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s=2$$\end{document}).

The corresponding stiffness matrix for the elastic foundation is given by:50\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} [K^f] = \frac{E_m I_0 (N+1)^3}{L^3} [K^{f*}] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$[K^{f*}]$$\end{document} is the dimensionless stiffness matrix of the foundation for the (DPM)51\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left[ {K_{}^{f*}} \right] = \left[ {\begin{array}{*{20}{c}} {\frac{{k_1^{sf*}}}{{{{(N + 1)}^4}}}}& 0& 0& 0& 0& \ldots & .& \ldots & 0& 0\\ 0& \ddots & 0& 0& 0& \ddots & .& .& .& .\\ 0& 0& \ddots & 0& 0& 0& 0& .& \vdots & \vdots \\ 0& 0& 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& 0& \vdots & \vdots \\ 0& 0& 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0& \vdots \\ .& .& 0& .& .& {\frac{{k_r^{sf*}}}{{{{(N + 1)}^4}}}}& \ddots & \ddots & 0& 0\\ \vdots & .& 0& \ddots & .& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0\\ .& \vdots & .& \ddots & 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0\\ .& .& .& .& .& 0& \ddots & \ddots & \ddots & 0\\ 0& 0& \ldots & .& .& 0& 0& 0& 0& {\frac{{k_N^{sf*}}}{{{{(N + 1)}^4}}}} \end{array}} \right] \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In summary, the dimensionless relationships between the dynamic properties of the continuous beam and those of the equivalent (DPM) are as follows: the stiffness of the spiral spring is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_r^* = D_{11r}^* - {(B_{11r}^*)^2}/{A_{11r}^*}$$\end{document}, the mass is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_r^* = I_{0r}^*$$\end{document}, and the foundation stiffness is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${k_r^{sf*}}/{(N + 1)^4}$$\end{document}.

Expression of Natural Vibration Frequency

In this section, we seek to establish the natural frequencies and mode shapes of the discrete physical model (DPM). By applying Eqs. 21, 48, and 50, we formulate the algebraic equation that defines the free vibration characteristics of the DPM.52\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( [K^*] - \omega _{sys}^2 \frac{S_0 \rho _m L^4}{E_m I_0 (N + 1)^4} [M^*] \right) \{ A \} = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The equation can be rewritten in the standard form for eigenvalue problems:53\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( [K^*] - \Omega ^* [M^*] \right) \{ A \} = 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Here, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega ^*$$\end{document} denotes the eigenvalue, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ A \}$$\end{document} represents the corresponding eigenvector. These values are determined by solving Eq. 53 with the reduced dimensionless mass and stiffness matrices, which are influenced by the boundary conditions.

The natural frequency of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r^{\text {th}}$$\end{document} vibrational mode in the discrete system can be computed with the following equation:54\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _{sys\,r} = \sqrt{\frac{E_m I_0}{\rho _m S_0 L^4}} (N + 1)^2 \sqrt{\Omega _r^*} \quad \text {for} \quad r = 1, \ldots , N \end{aligned}$$\end{document}The dimensionless frequency is determined by :55\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _{sys\,r}^* = \omega _{sys\,r} L^2 \sqrt{\frac{\rho _m S_0}{E_m I_0}} \quad \text {for} \quad r = 1, \ldots , N \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Inserting Eq. 54 into Eq. 55 yields the following expression for the dimensionless frequency of the discrete physical model (DPM).56\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _{sys\,r}^* = (N + 1)^2 \sqrt{\Omega _r^*} \quad \text {for} \quad r = 1, \ldots , N \end{aligned}$$\end{document}To ensure convergence and stability, it is recommended that the discretization method maintains a degree of freedom value of at least 100.

Validation of results

Consider a numerical example of a non-uniform functionally graded material (FGM) beam. The natural frequency is normalised for this comparison as follows :57\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _{\textrm{sys}}^* = \omega _{\textrm{sys}} L^2 \sqrt{\frac{\rho _0 S_0}{E_0 I_0}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}In this formulation, L represents the length of the beam, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^*_{\textrm{sys}}$$\end{document} denotes the dimensionless frequency parameter. The parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_0$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _0$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_0$$\end{document} refer to the geometric and material properties at the section located at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x = 0$$\end{document}.

To address this problem, a discrete physical model (DPM) is employed. In the current study, the number of masses N was varied between 40 and 200 to evaluate its impact on the accuracy of the vibrational analysis. It was observed that when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 100$$\end{document}, the results stabilised and did not change significantly with further increases in N. Therefore, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 100$$\end{document} is considered the optimal number of masses for this model.

This indicates that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 100$$\end{document} strikes a balance between computational efficiency and result accuracy. A lower number of masses (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N < 100$$\end{document}) led to less accurate results, while increasing N beyond 100 did not improve accuracy. As a result, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N = 100$$\end{document} is deemed sufficient to ensure convergence and accuracy in this study. This finding aligns with the general principle that a sufficient number of masses are needed to capture the system’s dynamics accurately, without unnecessarily increasing computational effort.

For the calculation of dimensionless frequencies, the number of degrees of freedom (N-DOF) is set to 100 in the MATLAB code. The results from this study are compared with those from24 , who investigated a non-uniform axially functionally graded (AFG) simply supported (SS) beam on a variable elastic foundation, and from18, who examined a uniform exponentially functionally graded material (FGM) beam. In this case, the material properties for the FGM beam are described by Eqs. 1 and 2, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y = 0$$\end{document}.

Comparative study 1

This comparison applies to a (SS) beam resting on partial foundations. The stiffness magnitude of the foundations is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_r^{2f*} = \eta (1 + \sin (\alpha x_r^*))$$\end{document}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_r^* = {r}/{(N+1)}$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = 1, \ldots , (N+1)\chi$$\end{document}, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document} representing the reduced length or position of the foundation. The rotational spring stiffness is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_r^* = (1 - 0.5 x_r^*)^2$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = 2, \ldots , N+1$$\end{document}, and the additional mass is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_r^* = 1$$\end{document}. In this case, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{1}$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{N+2}$$\end{document} are set to zero.

The results are presented in Tables 1 and 2 for various foundation locations under simply supported (SS) boundary conditions. The frequencies obtained from the (DPM) are highly consistent with those derived using the Hencky bar-chain model, as presented in24.

The results for boundary conditions (CC), (CS), and (CF) are not covered in24. Tables 3, 4, and 5 illustrate the impact of parameters such as foundation intensity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}, location \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha$$\end{document} on the non-dimensional frequencies for these boundary conditions.

Figure 5 presents the first four normalized mode shapes of the simply supported (SS) beam for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 0.7$$\end{document} for three values of the foundation stiffness, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = 10$$\end{document}, 100 and 1000. For the first mode, the influence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document} is marked : as the foundation becomes stiffer, the deformation is progressively shifted and concentrated towards the right part of the span, while the response near the left end is strongly reduced. The second mode also exhibits a noticeable sensitivity to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document}, with clear changes in the relative amplitudes of its positive and negative lobes. In contrast, the third and fourth modes are almost unchanged when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta$$\end{document} increases, indicating that the higher modes are only weakly affected by the Comparative study 2

In this section, a comparative analysis is performed on the dimensionless frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w^* = w L^2 \sqrt{{(E_m I_0)}/{(r_m S_0)}}$$\end{document} with the data presented in18 across various boundary conditions. The analysis focuses on a uniform beam characterized by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^*_r = 1$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^*_r = 1$$\end{document}, which is not influenced by any foundation (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = 0$$\end{document}). Parameters for mass \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m^*_r$$\end{document} and the coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C^*_r$$\end{document} are calculated using Eqs. 22 and 40 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y = 0$$\end{document}, representing an axial functional gradation (AFG). The results are summarized in Table 6, which highlights the first six dimensionless frequencies of the (AFG) beam under different boundary conditions, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x = 3$$\end{document}. These frequencies are compared to resonance frequencies obtained using the finite element method (FEM) as reported in18. The findings demonstrate a strong correlation between the proposed model and the FEM results, regardless of the boundary conditions, indicating that the current discrete model accurately estimates the Special case of a non-uniform exponentially AFG Beam on an elastic foundation

This section examines a specific case involving a non-uniform axially functionally graded (AFG) beam with an exponentially tapered profile, resting on an elastic foundation. The beam has a variable height defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h(x) = h_0 \exp (-0.1x/L)$$\end{document} and a constant width \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b(x)=b_0$$\end{document}. Consequently, the non-dimensional mass magnitude is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_r^* = \exp (-0.4x_r^*)$$\end{document}, and the non-dimensional flexibility of the beam is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_r^* = \exp (-0.4x_r^*)$$\end{document}.

The beam is subjected to various boundary conditions, including clamped-clamped (CC), clamped-simply supported (CS), simply supported-simply supported (SS), and clamped-free (CF).

Dimensionless frequencies of an exponentially tapered beam under various boundary conditions, with a foundation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_r^{1f*} = \eta (1 - \alpha x_r^*)^2$$\end{document}, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 1$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 3$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = 100$$\end{document}.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 0.5$$\end{document} (Table 7), the first mode frequency (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{*}_{sys\,1}$$\end{document}) is highest under the (CC) boundary condition (24.8948), indicating that clamped ends provide greater stiffness and result in higher frequencies. Conversely, the (SS) boundary condition yields the lowest frequency (14.1442), reflecting increased flexibility. The higher mode frequencies show significant increases, with the third mode (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega ^{*}_{sys\,3}$$\end{document}) reaching 122.4105 for the (CC) condition.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 1$$\end{document} (Table 8), the first mode frequencies increase across all boundary conditions compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 0.5$$\end{document}, with the CC boundary condition again exhibiting the highest frequency (27.2254). This increase suggests that a fully supported foundation enhances stiffness, leading to higher frequencies. The trend observed in the higher modes is consistent with the partial foundation case, with all frequencies increasing.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 0.5$$\end{document} (Table 9), the first mode frequencies are lower across all boundary conditions compared to those in Tables 7 and 8, with the highest value under the CC condition (22.7047). This reduction in frequency is due to the parabolic foundation, which provides less stiffness compared to the sinusoidal foundation.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi = 1$$\end{document} (Table 10), the frequencies increase again for all boundary conditions, with the (CC) boundary condition yielding the highest frequency (27.2254). The parabolic foundation continues to produce lower frequencies than the sinusoidal foundation, demonstrating that the type of foundation significantly impacts the vibrational characteristics.

Numerical results and discussions

Upon validation of the proposed model, it will be employed to investigate the free vibration characteristics of non-uniform (2D-FGM) beams featuring diverse profiles and boundary conditions in the presence of a foundation.

Uniform (2D-FGM ) beam without elastic foundation

This section focuses on computing the dimensionless frequencies for a uniform beam made from a two-dimensional functionally graded material (2D-FGM), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_r^* = b_r^* = 1$$\end{document}, under various gradient indices (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}). The beam is subjected to different boundary conditions, including clamped-free (CF), clamped-clamped (CC), and simply supported-simply supported (SS).

Tables 11, 12, and 13 present the dimensionless frequencies of the first three vibration modes of the beam under the (SS), (CC), and (CF) boundary conditions, respectively. The values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _r^*$$\end{document} for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r = 1, 2,$$\end{document} and 3 are provided for increasing values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}.

For all boundary conditions, the frequencies decrease with increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}. For instance, when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y = 0.2$$\end{document}, the frequency drops from 10.82 to 4.63 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} increases from 0.2 to 8. Similarly, frequencies also decrease with increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} for a fixed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document}. For example, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x = 0.2$$\end{document}, the frequency decreases from 10.82 to 4.63 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} increases from 0.2 to 8.

The overall trend indicates that the vibrational frequencies decrease with both \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}. This behavior can be attributed to the increase in the structure’s mass and rigidity. Specifically, as the grading indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} increase, the effective mass of the beam rises, which is similar to adding mass to a spiral spring system. In this analogy, when additional mass is introduced, the stiffness of the spring becomes less effective at resisting displacement, leading to lower vibrational frequencies.

The power indices significantly influence the distribution of material properties, which in turn affects the stiffness characteristics of the beam. As the indices increase, the material transitions toward a configuration that is softer, analogous to a spiral spring that has been stretched. This results in modifications to the beam’s stiffness matrix, where the effective spring constants decrease, further contributing to the reduction in vibrational frequencies.

An interesting observation is that, for a fixed value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}, a variation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} leads to slight frequency variations, whereas for a fixed value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document}, an increase in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} results in more significant frequency changes, as illustrated in Fig. 6. This suggests that the influence of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} is more pronounced, possibly due to its role in modulating the vertical stiffness, while \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} affects the horizontal gradient and mass distribution to a lesser extent.

This nuanced response underscores the complexity of the interaction between the beam’s geometric and material properties. Changes in the indices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document} not only alter the material composition but also significantly Non-uniform (2D-FGM) without any foundation

This subsection presents the vibrational behavior of a (2D-FGM) tapered beam, with mechanical properties defined previously in Eqs. 1 and 2. Various parameters, such as the index parameters (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x$$\end{document}, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y$$\end{document}, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document}) for material distribution and non-uniformity, are considered.

Several example problems involving non-uniform geometries are examined to demonstrate the simplicity of using (DPM) for analyzing the vibration frequencies of (2D-FGM) beams with a non-uniform profile. Two types of beam profiles are considered, as shown in Fig 7.

The thickness of the beam, h(x), as a function of x, can be expressed using the equations for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1(x)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_2(x)$$\end{document}. The thickness variation (decreasing-increasing), shown in Fig 7-a, is defined by: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_1(x) = h_{\text {max}} \left[ 1 - \gamma \left( 1 - \left| 1 - \frac{2x}{L} \right| \right) ^n \right] ,$$\end{document} and the thickness variation (increasing-decreasing), shown in Fig 7-b, is defined by: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_2(x) = h_{\text {max}} \left[ 1- \gamma \left| 1 - \frac{2x}{L} \right| ^n \right] ,$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h_{\text {max}}$$\end{document} representing the maximum height chosen as the reference height in this study. In the frequency calculation, the non-dimensional height presented in Eqs. 22, 41, 42, and 43 can be given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^*_{r1} = \left[ 1 - \gamma \left( 1 - \left| 1 - 2x_r^* \right| \right) ^n \right] ,$$\end{document} or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h^*_{2r} = \left[ 1- \gamma \left| 1 - 2x_r^* \right| ^n \right] ,$$\end{document} with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_r^* = 1$$\end{document}.

Each variation in the thickness of the beam can follow either a linear \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=1)$$\end{document} or parabolic \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=2)$$\end{document} profile, as illustrated in Fig 8. These variations are assumed to be symmetric about the beam’s mid-span (L/2).

The graphs in Figs. 9 and 10 present the first three dimensionless frequencies for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n_x=3)$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n_y=4)$$\end{document} as a function of the taper parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} for the boundary conditions (SS), (CC), (CS), and (CF).

Fig 9 shows the frequencies for a (decreasing-increasing) taper profile, while Fig 10 shows the frequencies for an (increasing-decreasing) taper profile. In both figures, the frequencies generally decrease as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} increases, regardless of the boundary conditions or the taper profile (linear with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=1$$\end{document} or parabolic with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n=2$$\end{document}). The (CC) boundary condition results in the highest frequencies, indicating the stiffest configuration, while the (CF) boundary condition produces the lowest frequencies due to increased flexibility. The (SS) and (CS) boundary conditions exhibit frequencies that fall between the (CC) and (CF) conditions, with the (CS) beam showing higher frequencies than the (SS) beam but lower than the (CC) beam.

The reduction in frequencies with increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} can be attributed to the increasing tapering of the beam, which leads to a reduction in the effective stiffness. As the taper parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} increases, the beam’s profile becomes more pronounced, resulting in a greater mass distribution towards the ends and a less rigid structure overall. This behavior can be interpreted in the context of a conical beam: as the taper increases, the flexibility of the beam can be modeled as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N+2$$\end{document} interconnected spiral springs, which collectively become less effective at resisting deformation, resulting in lower vibrational frequencies.

For linear and parabolic conicity profiles, beams with parabolic conicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=2)$$\end{document} exhibit slightly higher frequencies than beams with linear conicity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n=1)$$\end{document} in both profiles. This indicates that the parabolic conicity profile significantly affects the dynamic behavior of the beam in both cases. These trends are consistent across two mode shapes (modes 2 and 3). In mode 1, however, the frequency remains virtually constant.

Fig 11 and Fig 12 illustrate the first four linear mode shapes of a parabolically (decreasing-increasing) simply supported (SS) beam and a clamped-clamped (CC) beam, respectively, for a range of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma$$\end{document} values. In both figures, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x = 3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y = 4$$\end{document}.

Fig 13 and Fig 14 present the first four linear mode shapes of a parabolically (decreasing-increasing) clamped-free (CF) beam and a clamped-supported (CS) beam, respectively, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 0$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = 0.8$$\end{document}. In both figures,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_x = 3$$\end{document} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_y = 4$$\end{document}.Fig. 7The profiles for the non-uniform beam : a) (decreasing-increasing), and b) (increasing-decreasing).Fig. 8The profiles for the non-uniform beam for various values of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym}
